# Sūrah Yūnus (Chapter 10) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gate to the Alif-Lām-Rā Hexalogy**

Sūrah Yūnus occupies a distinct and strategically pivotal position within the architecture of the Qur'anic canon. Numbered as the tenth chapter in the standard ʿUthmānic codex (*muṣḥaf*), it serves as the grand portal to an unbroken sequence of six sūrahs—Chapters 10 through 15: Yūnus, Hūd, Yūsuf, al-Raʿd, Ibrāhīm, and al-Ḥijr—that all open with the mystical, resonant formula of the disconnected letters *Alif, Lām, Rāʾ* (with the singular, deliberate variation of *Alif, Lām, Mīm, Rāʾ* in Sūrah al-Raʿd). This placement marks a profound thematic shift in the reader's journey through scripture. Following the vast legislative, communal, and constitutional landscapes of the great Madinan chapters (al-Baqarah, Āl ʿImrān, al-Nisāʾ, al-Māʾidah, and al-Tawbah), Sūrah Yūnus re-immerses the soul into the concentrated, primordial crucible of Makkan preaching. It redirects the human gaze from the external ordinances of the state and communal law back to the foundational, existential questions of existence: the reality of transcendent revelation, the living signs written into the cosmos, the psychological anatomy of human arrogance and ingratitude, the certainty of the Final Return (*al-Maʿād*), and the absolute, unshared sovereignty of the Creator.

The consensus of classical scholarship—represented by authorities such as Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī, Mujāhid, and Qatādah, and codified by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr—affirms that Sūrah Yūnus was revealed almost entirely during the middle to late Makkan era, prior to the Prophet's ﷺ historic migration (*Hijrah*) to Madinah. While certain commentators, such as al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī, record minority reports suggesting that verses 40 and 94–96 may have descended or been reiterated in Madinah, the rhythmic cadence, rhetorical intensity, and theological focus of the chapter exhibit an unmistakable Makkan unity. It was revealed in an environment where a beleaguered, persecuted prophetic community confronted a powerful pagan oligarchy entrenched in ancestral idolatry, tribal supremacy, and fatalistic skepticism toward the afterlife. The sūrah does not appeal to physical coercion or political leverage; rather, it deploys an overwhelming array of rational proofs, cosmic observations, psychological analyses, and sacred historical precedents to dismantle the edifice of polytheism and awaken the dormant human intellect.

**The Epistemological Core: Revelation, Wisdom, and the Repudiation of Conjecture**

At the very heart of Sūrah Yūnus lies an uncompromising epistemic defense of divine revelation (*al-Waḥy*) and a systematic demolition of unverified human conjecture (*al-ẓann*). The pagan Makkans greeted the Prophet Muhammad's ﷺ mission with mocking astonishment, questioning how the Transcendent Lord of the universe could communicate through a mortal man walking among them in the marketplace (10:2). They dismissed the Qur'an alternatively as poetic fabrication, ancestral mythology, or manifest sorcery (*siḥr mubīn*). The sūrah systematically refutes each of these claims, establishing that the Qur'an is the uncreated speech of the Creator, impossible to be manufactured by any human or demonic agency:

> *"And this Qur'an is not such as could ever be produced by other than God; but it is a confirmation of that which was before it and a detailed explanation of the Book, wherein there is no doubt, from the Lord of the worlds."* (Qur'an 10:37)

To demonstrate this reality beyond all reasonable doubt, the sūrah issues its famous open challenge (*al-Taḥaddī*): if skeptics maintain that this text is of mortal composition, let them gather all the literary geniuses, philosophers, and supernatural forces across history and compose a single chapter resembling its sublime eloquence, moral depth, and cognitive coherence (10:38). The sūrah demonstrates that human resistance to truth is rarely the result of honest intellectual deficit; rather, it is the byproduct of *al-ẓann*—the uncritical reliance upon cultural assumptions, ancestral prejudices, and wishful thinking:

> *"And most of them follow naught but conjecture. Truly conjecture avails nothing against the truth."* (Qur'an 10:36)

**The Grand Triad: Cosmic Origin (Mabdaʾ), Perpetual Governance (Tadbīr), and the Final Return (Maʿād)**

The theological architecture of Sūrah Yūnus is structured upon a magnificent triad that encompasses the entire arc of created existence: the Origin (*al-Mabdaʾ*), Active Governance (*al-Tadbīr*), and the Final Assembly (*al-Maʿād*). The physical cosmos is presented not as a self-originating accident or an abandoned mechanical clock, but as a purposeful, exquisitely calibrated sanctuary brought into existence in six cosmic epochs (*sittati ayyām*), after which the Creator established His majesty upon the Supreme Throne (*al-ʿArsh*), actively directing every single subatomic, biological, and celestial affair (*yudabbiru al-amr*).

The sūrah draws human contemplation to the celestial canopy—the sun engineered as an intense, self-generating radiance (*ḍiyāʾ*) and the moon as a gentle, reflective light (*nūr*) whose calibrated orbital stations (*manāzil*) enable humanity to compute time and structure civilization (10:5). The perpetual alternation of night and day, the ecological cycle of rain and vegetation, and the delicate balance of atmospheric systems are presented as dynamic, living testimonies to divine unity (*Tawḥīd*). From this cosmological foundation, the sūrah builds an inescapable logical deduction: the Omnipotent Being Who initiated this vast cosmic order *ex nihilo* possesses the self-evident power to resurrect decayed human bones and reconstitute human consciousness for moral reckoning. The Resurrection is not merely physically possible; it is an absolute moral necessity demanded by divine justice (*al-qisṭ*):

> *"Unto Him is your return all together; God’s Promise is true. Verily He originates creation, then He brings it back, that He may recompense with justice those who believe and perform righteous deeds."* (Qur'an 10:4)

**The Anatomy of Human Psychology in Prosperity and Peril**

A signature brilliance of Sūrah Yūnus is its piercing, forensic exploration of human spiritual psychology. The chapter provides timeless case studies of human fickleness, cognitive dissonance, and spiritual amnesia. In moments of extreme vulnerability—such as when a human being is struck by agonizing illness, or when sailors in a ship are suddenly engulfed by towering, mountainous waves on a pitch-black, tempestuous sea—all secondary dependencies, worldly arrogance, and fabricated idols evaporate. The human soul instinctively accesses its primordial covenant (*al-Fiṭrah*), crying out to the One True God with undivided sincerity and desperate vows of lifelong gratitude (10:12, 22). Yet, the very instant divine mercy delivers them safely to dry land, they relapse into arrogance, injustice, and secular rebellion (*baghy*) upon the earth (10:23).

To shatter this persistent illusion of worldly self-sufficiency, the sūrah presents the haunting agricultural parable of earthly life (*mathal al-ḥayāti al-dunyā*): an earth flourishing with lush, radiant vegetation, appearing invincible and fully mastered by its human cultivators, suddenly reduced to a barren, mown wasteland overnight by an instantaneous divine decree (10:24). In stark, luminous contrast to this fleeting mirage, God issues an open, universal invitation to the only permanent sanctuary of ultimate tranquility:

> *"And God calls unto the Abode of Peace (Dār al-Salām), and guides whomsoever He will unto a straight path."* (Qur'an 10:25)

**The Historical Cycle and the Singular Redemption of the People of Jonah**

The latter half of the sūrah grounds these theological and psychological realities in sacred historical narratives. It presents the unwavering, fearless confrontation of Prophet Noah (*Nūḥ*) against an entire hostile civilization (10:71–73), and the dramatic confrontation between Moses (*Mūsā*), Aaron (*Hārūn*), and the totalitarian military state of Pharaoh (10:75–93). It documents the tragic, irreversible doom of Pharaoh, who attempted to utter a desperate deathbed confession of faith only as the surging waters of the Red Sea collapsed upon his lungs—a confession rejected because it came after the threshold of moral agency had closed (10:90–92).

Contrasting sharply with the ruins of drowned empires is the unique, redemptive history of the people of Prophet Jonah (*Yūnus*), after whom the sūrah is named:

> *"Why has not a single town believed and benefitted from its faith, except the people of Jonah? When they believed, We removed from them the punishment of disgrace in the life of this world, and We granted them enjoyment for a time."* (Qur'an 10:98)

This historical exception serves as a monumental message of hope to the Quraysh and to all subsequent generations: historical destiny is not fatalistic. Sincere, collective repentance, undertaken before the physical descent of destruction, has the power to shatter historical precedents, avert catastrophe, and restore divine favor.

**The Methodology and Scope of This Expanded Commentary**

What follows is an exhaustive, verse-by-verse expanded commentary upon all 109 verses of Sūrah Yūnus. Each verse is approached as an independent, fully realized discourse. In accordance with classical scholarly standards, each section integrates:
1. Precise Arabic linguistic analysis, examining roots, morphology, syntactic subtleties, and rhetorical devices;
2. Authoritative Qur'anic cross-references, providing full scriptural parallels in translation;
3. Authentic Prophetic traditions (*Aḥādīth*) with collection sources and narrative contexts fully cited;
4. Classical exegetical insights from master commentators across the centuries, including al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Bayḍāwī, and Ibn Taymiyyah;
5. Rigorous theological (*ʿAqīdah*), philosophical, psychological, and sociological reflections;
6. Practical life lessons and relatable modern analogies designed to connect this eternal revelation to the contemporary human condition.

---
## Sūrah Yūnus 10:1

> **Alif. Lām. Rā. These are the signs of the Wise Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Luminous Letters and the Threshold of Sacred Speech**

The chapter opens not with an ordinary discursive statement, but with three isolated, spelled-out letters of the Arabic alphabet: *Alif. Lām. Rā.* (*al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*). These mysterious combinations appear at the head of twenty-nine chapters of the Qur'an, functioning as an immediate auditory and spiritual threshold. When a listener encounters them, the normal flow of human communication is momentarily arrested. Rather than hearing conventional syntax, the ear receives the raw acoustic material of speech itself—sound before sentence, phoneme before proposition. From the earliest generation of Islam, these letters evoked profound reverence and scholarly contemplation. It is transmitted from the first Caliph, Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq:

> *"In every revealed scripture there is a divine mystery (sirr), and the mystery of the Qur'an resides in the opening letters of the sūrahs."*

And from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib:

> *"Verily, every book possesses a quintessence (ṣafwah), and the quintessence of this Book is its spelled-out letters."*

Classical exegetes, led by Ibn ʿAbbās and followed across the centuries by al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr, highlight that these letters serve as a direct, unassailable demonstration of the Qur'an's miraculous inimitability (*iʿjāz*). The revelation was addressed to a society that had reached the absolute zenith of Arabic eloquence, oral poetry, and rhetorical mastery. The tribes of Arabia judged human excellence by the power of the spoken word. By opening sūrah after sūrah with the very consonants, vowels, and phonemes that formed the vocabulary of their daily transactions and poetic odes, the divine voice presents an inescapable challenge: here are the letters of your own language—letters you have known since childhood—yet from these identical phonetic building blocks, neither your greatest poets, nor your eloquent orators, nor the collective intellect of creation can fashion even a single chapter comparable to this Book. It is as though a master architect laid raw clay before rival builders and demonstrated that from that same clay, only divine craftsmanship could erect an unshakeable cathedral of truth.

**The Demonstrative of Elevation: "These Are the Signs"**

Immediately following the disconnected letters, the verse declares: *"These are the signs of the Wise Book"* (*Tilka āyātu al-Kitābi al-ḥakīm*). In classical Arabic grammar, the demonstrative pronoun *tilka* is the feminine singular pronoun used to point toward entities that are distant (*li'l-buʿd*). Yet here, it points to the verses of the chapter being recited in the immediate presence of the listener. Grammarians such as Sībawayh and commentators like al-Rāzī explain that this grammatical distance is an indicator of exalted rank, transcendent majesty, and sublime origin (*li-ʿuluwwi al-martabah*). When an orator gestures toward an honored king standing nearby and says "That great leader," the gesture of distance expresses not spatial separation, but ontological veneration. The text immediately establishes that these verses belong to a realm far above ordinary human discourse.

The verses are named *āyāt* (singular *āyah*), a term rich in cosmological and theological meaning. An *āyah* is not merely a linguistic sentence or a literary verse; it is an unmistakable signpost, an evident proof, and a miraculous indicator that points beyond itself to the majesty, wisdom, and presence of its Originator. Just as an astronomer reads the positions of distant stars as signs pointing to the gravitational laws of the universe, and a desert tracker reads footprints in the sand as undeniable evidence of a traveler who passed by, the reflective human soul reads each *āyah* of the Qur'an as an intellectual and spiritual landmark guiding the consciousness toward the Living God.

**The Tripartite Wisdom of Al-Kitāb Al-Ḥakīm**

The Book is crowned with the profound attribute *al-Ḥakīm* ("the Wise"). In Arabic morphology, *ḥakīm* is an intensive adjective (*ṣifah mushabbahah*) derived from the root *ḥ-k-m*, which carries a threefold convergence of meaning that classical scholars have analyzed in exhaustive detail:

1. **Precision of Construction (*Iḥkām*):** The Qur'an is described as *muḥkam*—flawlessly constructed, structurally unified, and immune to decay, internal contradiction, or factual error. As Ibn Kathīr notes in his monumental *Tafsīr*:
> *"It is decisive, clear, containing no falsehood from before it or behind it, revealed by the One who is All-Wise and Praiseworthy."*
Over twenty-three years of revelation, delivered amidst extreme historical upheavals, warfare, personal bereavement, and social transformation, the text maintains a seamless theological and moral coherence that defies mortal human composition.

2. **Profound Wisdom of Guidance (*Ḥikmah*):** Every command, prohibition, historical narrative, and cosmic parable contained within its pages is calibrated with supreme wisdom. It places every legal ruling and ethical principle in its exact, appropriate place, maximizing human flourishing (*maṣlaḥah*) in this world and guaranteeing spiritual salvation in the Hereafter. It does not burden the human soul with impossible asceticism, nor does it abandon it to hedonistic chaos; rather, it provides a perfectly balanced spiritual ecology for human life.

3. **Judicial Authority (*Ḥākim*):** The Book functions as the ultimate, incorruptible judge and arbiter over all human disputes, philosophical speculations, and moral dilemmas. It distinguishes between truth (*al-ḥaqq*) and falsehood (*al-bāṭil*), justice (*al-ʿadl*) and tyranny (*al-ẓulm*). When human cultures drift into ethical confusion, *al-Kitāb al-Ḥakīm* stands as the eternal, immovable standard against which all human ideologies must be measured.

**The Book as a Living Sanctuary in an Age of Confusion**

For the contemporary seeker, the opening verse of Sūrah Yūnus offers an indispensable intellectual anchor. Modern civilization is marked by epistemic fragmentation, information saturation, and pervasive moral relativism, where truth is often reduced to subjective feeling or political power. In this turbulent landscape, the Qur'an presents itself not as a human hypothesis or a cultural relic of antiquity, but as *al-Kitāb al-Ḥakīm*—a repository of objective, transcendent wisdom engineered by the Creator of human consciousness.

Just as physical laws like gravity operate with mathematical precision regardless of human consensus, the spiritual and moral laws revealed in the Wise Book operate with immutable certainty. To align one's life with this Book is to build one's existence upon solid bedrock; to ignore it is to build upon shifting sands. The verse invites the reader to step across the threshold of the mysterious letters, abandon epistemic pride, and enter into a transformative dialogue with the wisdom of the Infinite.

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:2

> **Is it a marvel for mankind that We have sent revelation unto a man from among themselves, [saying], “Warn mankind and give glad tidings to those who believe that they shall have a station of sincerity with their Lord”? The disbelievers say, “Surely this is a manifest sorcerer.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Anatomy of Human Astonishment: The Paradox of Prophetic Selection**

The verse opens with an interrogative particle of profound reprimand and astonishment (*istifhām inkārī wa-taʿajjubī*): *"Is it a marvel for mankind that We have sent revelation unto a man from among themselves...?"* (*A-kāna li'l-nāsi ʿajaban an awḥaynā ilā rajulin minhum*). Classical exegetes, including al-Ṭabarī, al-Wāḥidī in his *Asbāb al-Nuzūl*, al-Baghawī, and Ibn Kathīr, record the historical circumstances that occasioned this revelation. When the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ proclaimed his divine commission in Makkah, calling humanity to abandon idolatry and submit to the One True God, the ruling chieftains of Quraysh expressed derisive astonishment. They argued that if the Almighty Creator were to communicate with humanity, He ought to have dispatched an awe-inspiring, winged angel, or chosen an exceptionally wealthy, politically dominant oligarch from the twin cities of Makkah or Ṭāʾif, scoffing: "Could God find no one to send except this impoverished orphan of the household of Abū Ṭālib?"

This reaction exposes a profound and perennial cognitive bias in human psychology: the persistent assumption that divine truth and spiritual authority must manifest through sensational, overwhelming, or aristocratic displays of worldly power. The Qur'an exposes the deep irrationality of this skepticism. Revelation descending upon a human being from among humanity is not a cause for marvel; it is the ultimate expression of divine mercy, pedagogical wisdom, and psychological fitness. Had God sent an angelic messenger, the purpose of moral emulation would have been fundamentally defeated. Mortals would have protested: "An angel does not experience biological hunger, sexual desire, physical fatigue, fear of death, or grief over lost loved ones; how can an angel serve as a practical moral model for human beings who must struggle daily against the impulses of the flesh?"

By choosing a human messenger—one who walked through the dusty markets, married, raised children, wept at the graves of his loved ones, bled when struck in battle, and demonstrated unshakeable moral integrity under extreme adversity—God provided humanity with a living, tangible, and emulable embodiment of divine guidance. As God declares in Sūrah al-Anʿām:

> *"And if We had made him an angel, We would have made him [in the form of] a man, and We would have covered them with that in which they cover themselves."* (Qur'an 6:9)

**The Dual Apostolic Mission: The Architecture of Warning and Glad Tidings**

The core mandate entrusted to the Prophet ﷺ is articulated in two complementary directives: *"Warn mankind and give glad tidings to those who believe..."* (*an andhiri al-nāsa wa-bashshiri alladhīna āmanū*). The scope and grammatical targeting of these two verbs reflect the profound universalism and pastoral care of the prophetic mission:
- **Universal Warning (*Indhār*):** The warning is directed broadly and unconditionally to *"mankind"* (*al-nās*) in its entirety. It is an urgent, compassionate alarm sounded to every human being, alerting them to the moral reality of the cosmos, the inescapable law of divine accountability, the destructive consequences of injustice, and the catastrophic peril of the Hellfire.
- **Particular Glad Tidings (*Tabshīr*):** The glad tidings of eternal felicity, divine pleasure, and cosmic elevation are addressed specifically to *"those who believe"* (*alladhīna āmanū*)—those who respond to the warning with sincere faith and moral transformation.

In Islamic theological anthropology, these two polarities correspond directly to the foundational psychological drivers of human action: *al-Khawf* (reverent fear) and *al-Rajāʾ* (elevating hope). A soul governed solely by fear collapses into spiritual paralysis, neurosis, and existential despair; a soul governed solely by unanchored hope drifts into moral complacency, antinomianism, and arrogance. The prophetic pedagogy balances these two wings with absolute precision, utilizing warning to dismantle human arrogance and glad tidings to inspire sustained virtuous action.

**The Station of Sincerity (Qadam Ṣidq): Three Dimensions of High Honor**

The believers are promised an extraordinary, poetic reward: they shall possess a *Qadam Ṣidq* ("a station of sincerity" or "a footstep of truth") with their Lord. The classical masters of *Tafsīr* have unpacked the rich semantic layers of this unique Qur'anic phrase across three interrelated dimensions:

1. **Precedence and Steadfastness in Virtue (*Salaf Ṣidq wa-Sabq*):** Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, and Qatādah explained that *qadam* literally denotes a footstep, signifying precedence in righteous action, an established historical track record of sacrifice, and unshakeable steadfastness upon the path of truth. Just as a seasoned mountain climber secures a firm, immovable foothold on a treacherous precipice, the believer's sincere faith secures a firm, eternal standing in the divine presence that will never slip on the Day of Resurrection.
2. **True, Immutable Reward (*Thawāb Ṣidq*):** Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and al-Bayḍāwī noted that *qadam* here refers to the eternal, abundant reward that God has promised to the faithful—a reward described as *ṣidq* (true/sincere) because it is grounded in the absolute, infallible promise of God that never fails, decays, or disappoints.
3. **The Prophetic Intercession (*al-Shafāʿah al-Kubrā*):** ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, Zayd ibn Aslam, and several early authorities interpreted *Qadam Ṣidq* as the intercession of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ himself. On that terrifying Day when all creation stands trembling in the vast plain of the Gathering, the Prophet ﷺ will be granted the *Maqām Maḥmūd* (the Station of Glorified Praise), placing his foot forward on behalf of the faithful to plead for their forgiveness and entry into Paradise. The Prophet ﷺ affirmed this exalted station in an authenticated tradition:

> *"I am the master of the children of Adam on the Day of Resurrection, and no boast; in my hand will be the banner of praise, and no boast; and there will be no prophet that day, Adam or otherwise, except under my banner."* (Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī 3148, graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

**The Stigmatizing Defense Mechanism: "Surely This Is a Manifest Sorcerer"**

Faced with the undeniable moral elevation of the Messenger ﷺ and the overwhelming linguistic majesty of the Qur'an, the disbelievers retreated into the ultimate ad hominem slander: *"The disbelievers say, 'Surely this is a manifest sorcerer'"* (*Qāla al-kāfirūna inna hādhā la-sāḥirun mubīn*).

Classical linguists and commentators observe a significant textual reading here: some reciters read *sāḥir* ("sorcerer," referring to the Prophet ﷺ), while others read *siḥr* ("sorcery," referring directly to the Qur'an). Both readings converge upon the same psychological phenomenon. When entrenched worldly elites are confronted with a transformative truth that threatens their social monopolies, unjust economic structures, and ancestral superstitions, and when they find themselves intellectually incapable of refuting its arguments, they inevitably resort to pathologizing the messenger. By labeling the Prophet a sorcerer and his speech sorcery, the Quraysh attempted to explain away the miraculous, heart-melting power of the Qur'an over the masses without conceding its divine origin. It was an admission of their own defeat: they acknowledged that his words possessed an irresistible, supernatural power, but chose to ascribe that power to demonic deception rather than divine grace.
